The paid run is the measure lane's, on Kimi K3.1 and then GLM 5.3 flash. Its reserve is $0.05 on flash and $0 on the Kimi subscription. (Closes #1484, Refs #1442) |
| 0.771.0 | 2026-10-26 | This is the harness for EXP-B3c (X-B3c), the experiment that decides whether a headless run should have the host enforce the checks the model declared. The question is how often a run stops while its own last check still prints FAIL. evals/stops_on_fail.py reads a session file, or every session under a run directory, and prints one row per root session with its task, its stops and the stops taken over a FAIL. A child's session is skipped. A stop is the end of a turn: an accepted `answer`, or else a reply that stopped with nothing after it but host notes before the next typed message or the end of the file. So a stop the host sent back, as the todo intercept does when todos are still open, is not taken, and the one that finally stands is. A held answer is not a stop, and a reply after an accepted answer is not a second one. A FAIL line is the verifier shape unattended.md asks for, `FAIL <requirement>` at the start of a line of the last ipython cell's stdout, so `FAILED to import` is not one. The fixtures are three sessions the real binary wrote under faux/faux-1 and scrubbed with `evals/record.py --scrub-only`. Two run two cells and then reply. One ends on `FAIL R2` after an all-PASS cell, the other the reverse, so a counter that reads any cell but the last gets both wrong. The third sets todos, stops over `FAIL R2`, is sent back by the todo intercept, runs an all-PASS cell, marks a todo done and stops again: one stop, none over a FAIL. No TB task text or transcript is committed. evals/kill/x-b3c.py is the experiment's kill-test module, ladder step 6. It runs main's binary on the 12-task slice in evals/drivers/tbv4_slice.txt, one fresh trial per task, on Kimi K3.1 and then z-ai/glm-5.3-flash, through `tbv4_sweep.sh --runner` with inference-net excluded. A task counts when any stop in its root session was taken over a FAIL. The verdict is KEEP when that happens in at least 2 of 12 tasks on each family, and KILL otherwise. KILL is read as soon as one family that finished all 12 is under 2, because KEEP needs every family. Otherwise it reads UNRESOLVED while a family has not finished all 12, with each family's count either way. The sessions are read from the harbor job directories this sweep filed trial rows from: trials.py files each row with its `job`, and kill.py's harbor row names the jobs of the rows its own sweep appended, so a re-run under the same run id reads each task's latest sweep and never sums two. A sweep refused before harbor starts files no rows and names no job, and that family reads 0 of 0. `--check` survives either verdict. Today kill.py writes a harbor run on the Kimi subscription unrun, because a sweep spends OpenRouter on its own model and the TB container has no Kimi credential. So the Kimi family reads unrun, and until the sweep can carry a Kimi route the verdict is KILL or UNRESOLVED, never KEEP. The paid run belongs to the measure lane and does not run here, so evals/trials/ns-x-b3c.jsonl is written by that run, not by this change. (Closes #1473, Refs #1315, Refs #1442) |
| 0.770.0 | 2026-10-26 | An armed plan wait was checked and then unblocked without carrying the checked plan revision into the operation. Two firings could both read the row as blocked; the first moved it to pending, and the second failed with an illegal unblock instead of holding. The same gap could let a stale firing unblock a newer wait. The unblock now binds to the revision returned by that read. A stale revision rereads the wait condition and retries, while a row that no longer waits on the address is reported as held. Competing firings therefore unblock the row once rather than racing into an illegal step or unblocking a different wait. (Refs #1442) |
| 0.769.0 | 2026-10-26 | This is Bet 1 item 4 of Yi's next shape, the reap half (B1-reap-address). A plan child's ending used to reach its owner as the verdict line plus a `<reaped_child>` block holding up to 2,000 chars of the child's last answer. The block rode every later request, and reclaim never cuts it, so twelve finished children cost about 24 KB on each turn after them. The verdict line now names where the product lives and carries none of it. An accepted child's line reads `plan: accepted "<todo>" (agent://<plan>/<todo>); its product is plan://<plan>/artifacts/<hex>`, the artifact the engine stored as the todo's output. A failed child's line reads `plan: failed "<todo>": <cause>; its last product is history://<plan>/<todo>`, the address the engine records as the failed todo's `last`, and a child the contract refuses or whose merge conflicts ends its `plan: refused` or `plan: failed` line the same way. When the todo has a contract, the list of checks the engine passed starts on its own line, so no period is glued to the product address. A reap outside a finish (an owner's `done` or `fail`) reports `reaped <child> (<status>): its transcript is history://<child>`, or says the child kept no transcript. `SubagentHost::harvests` is now the set of children whose finish is settling, so their reap reports nothing beside the verdict. The zero-spend kill test asked that every address a finish line prints open in `read`, `grep` and `rlm.fetch`. `read` and `rlm.fetch` opened all three forms. `grep` opened only `history://`: it handed `plan://` and `agent://` to the file grep, which refused them as paths that do not exist. The URL grep now serves any address `read` takes as one: `history://` is still searched by entry, an `agent://` address that has a reap pin is searched as the `history://` transcript it pins, its rows naming entries `read` opens, and any other address is fetched through the resolver and searched by line, its rows named `<address> line <n>:` so the leading token is the address `read` opens, with the same 40-hit page and 200-char line cuts the history grep names, each with its own notice. YI_DESIGN.md section 13.3 and the plan row of the ARCHITECTURE feature ledger say the same. The runtime crate grows by 33 lines against this PR's base, not the -8 the plan estimated: the address grep the kill test required is the difference. Tests grow by 297 lines, not the 70 estimated: two plan_readers tests over a root wired as `yi` wires one (its plan tool, its URL-routed `read` and `grep`, its kernel), whose wiring helper is about 45 of those lines, and one fetch_grep test for the address grep's two cuts at their limits, one past each alone, and one past both. (D487; Closes #1489, Refs #1442) |
| 0.768.0 | 2026-10-26 | Preserve a kernel cut row through the shared output view only for shell cells without a spill pointer. Background job output that merely starts with the same prefix stays in the ordinary middle cut, and regression tests cover both paths. (Refs #1498) |
| 0.767.0 | 2026-10-26 | Named kill tests now run against a built yi through one runner, evals/kill.py, under one spend ledger shared by every worktree at ~/Development/yi-runs/next-shape/ledger.jsonl. Each test is its own module under evals/kill/, so the units that own tests never edit a shared registry. A family starts only if the dollars spent, the open reserves, the reserves of earlier ladder steps not yet settled and its own reserve stay within $30. A family that cannot fit is written unrun with the dollars it needed, and `report` lists every test with its verdict or that figure. Every run goes through one budgeted ask. It gets a fresh HOME seeded with a clone of one prebuilt kernel venv, a fresh shared git clone as its cwd, and a Kimi access token without the refresh token, and the HOME and the clone are deleted once its row is written. The stream is killed live when its cost crosses what is left, and a run with an unpriced turn is charged its ceiling. An exhausted Kimi quota parks the Kimi runs until the window resets, and a reset past the test's deadline writes the run unrun with its estimated cost. The routes live in evals/kill/providers.json. `yi catalog refresh openrouter` confirmed z-ai/glm-5.3-flash, z-ai/glm-5.3, deepseek/deepseek-v4.1-flash and moonshotai/kimi-k3.1-pro on 2026-10-24. The OpenRouter Kimi route ships disabled, and the Kimi subscription route stays unconfirmed until the Kimi login's live checks name the K3.1 id. `yi login <provider> --refresh <seconds>` refreshes through the native profile-aware OAuth flow and prints an access-token file with no refresh token, or refuses when the token cannot cover the requested lifetime. The kill runner shells out to it instead of keeping a second refresh grant and token-store writer. (D486; Refs #1462, Refs #1442) |
| 0.766.0 | 2026-10-26 | Dead items in six leaf crates are deleted, each one found again at the fork before it was touched. The compaction window chain is gone: yi-context loses `Window`, the runtime's `Compactor` loses its window state and is built with `Compactor::default()`, and a compaction entry's `details` no longer gets a `window` key. Entries already on disk keep the key, and `CompactionDetails.window` stays in yi-types, so they decode and re-emit it byte for byte; a new fixture holds one the compactor wrote. `SourceBudgets.ledger`, which nothing read, goes too. In the loop, the `added_tool_names` filter (every constructor sets `None`) becomes a plain `None`, and lib.rs stops re-exporting `repair_tool_name`, `STREAM_RETRY_AT`, `STREAM_RETRY_TEXT` and `REPEAT_BREAK_TEXT`; the `ReasoningBudget` re-export the plan listed was already absent at the fork, so nothing changed for it. The `temperature` option of `AnthropicOptions` and `OpenAiOptions` goes with its three request-body sites and the `supportsTemperature` compat key on the three Opus catalog rows. D306 listed that key among the two compat keys kept; a catalog file written earlier that still names it loads and is ignored. `fib_dir`, `finalize_frame` and `radius_scale` become crate-private (`Orb::showing` stays: a test added after the fork seeds its orb with it). In ACP, `_yi/kernel_cancel` (no client sends it) now answers as an unknown method, together with `UserCells::cancel` and the cell's `call_id` that only it read. The `ToolExecutionUpdate` projection arm goes (no producer emits that event; it now projects to no update), and so does `daemon_protocol_version`. The `ToolCallContentChunk` variant of `AcpSessionUpdate` stays in yi-types: nothing builds it any more, but without it the enum's largest variant is 288 bytes against 72 for the next, which fails clippy's large-enum-variant rule, and boxing `ToolCallUpdate` instead would rewrite eight patterns in the console. In permission, `needs_host` goes (D312 names it; the runtime's `leaves_sandbox` has applied the same rule through `host_need`), and `ripwire_reads` is private. The method table in design section 17.2 drops `_yi/kernel_cancel`. (D485; Closes #1501, Refs #1442) |
| 0.765.0 | 2026-10-26 | This is Bet 2's kernel capture fix (unit B2-kernel-headtail). It revises D316's list of producers that cut with no file: a displayed value (`execute_result`) past the kernel's cut now spills like a stream. The kernel's model-facing capture of a cell's stdout and stderr keeps its 65,536-char budget, but as the first 32,768 chars and the last 32,768 instead of the first 65,536. A chunk that lands exactly on the cap no longer stops the capture: every char is counted, so text after it is cut with a row instead of dropped with none. The held text is trimmed back to its two ends once it passes eight times the cap in bytes, so a long cell holds at most about 512 KiB per stream. A displayed value is capped the same way. Every cut writes one row between the two ends that names the chars kept, the total, `DEFAULT_MAX_OUTPUT_CHARS 65536`, and the full output file named below it. The displayed value now reaches `CellSpill` through the kernel's uncapped `on_stream` hook under the name `execute_result`, so a value past the cap is kept whole in the cell's spill file and the cut names `[full output: path]`. The spill file holds the cell's whole output: its stdout, its stderr and its displayed value. A `%%bash` or `!cmd` cell's view, when it has no file to name, ends with any kernel cut row its own middle cut dropped, so the first cut stays named. The ipython tool description drops "a displayed value past that is cut with no file" and says the three keep their first and last halves. (D484; Closes #1498, Refs #1442) |
| 0.764.0 | 2026-10-25 | This is the usage fold (unit HA-TOK-fold). A goal's token budget stopped counting fresh tokens once a conversation had a long cached prefix. The goal subtracted a reply's cache reads from its input, but the providers already report `Usage.input` without them (the Anthropic mapper copies `input_tokens`, which leaves out cached and cache-written tokens, and the OpenAI mappers take the cached tokens off the prompt count themselves). With 500 fresh input tokens and 9,000 cache reads, the subtraction went negative, was clamped to zero, and the reply counted for its 300 output tokens alone. A goal with a budget of 1,000 tokens needed four such replies to cross it instead of two, and the longer the cached prefix, the later it crossed. `Usage` now has two named folds in `yi-types`, so there is one place that says how a usage block reads as tokens. `fresh` is input plus cache writes plus output, what the reply newly cost, and the goal's accounting reads it where it used to call its own `usage_delta`, which is gone. `context` is input plus cache reads plus cache writes, what the request held whether it came from cache or not. The TUI's input tally (the turn footer's `N in` and `N% cached`, and the status bar's session rate) now reads it instead of its own signed sum, which let a negative input eat the cache reads it was summed with and showed `400% cached`; the child and live-context units that follow name it as their input too. Each part is clamped at zero before the sum and the sum saturates, because a live usage chunk can map to a negative `input`: an OpenAI-shaped upstream whose `prompt_tokens` is below the cache counts it reports beside it, which the mapper subtracts from the prompt. A negative part must not take a real count with it. No wire shape moves: `Usage` and `Goal` serialize as before, and a goal's stored `tokens_used` is only counted differently from here on. A goal stored by an older build keeps the lower count it had. One producer is not net of cache reads: the OpenRouter generation record that settles a cut turn stores the whole prompt in `input` and the cached part beside it in `cache_read`. That usage reaches the store's `settled` rows and, for a child, its parent's last message through the settle hook the child is wired with, but never the goal, which folds only the live reply. A later fold over those rows (the child-token and stats units) has to take `cache_read` off `input` first, or the record should store it net, as the stream mappers do. (Closes #1503, Refs #1442) |
| 0.763.0 | 2026-10-25 | This is Bet 1 item 1 of Yi's next shape (B1-reader-route): the Reader half of #937. A plan delegation used to spawn as a full root child whatever it asked for, so a Python `Reader` declared through `plan.todo(delegate=Reader(partition=[...]))` was sent the root's whole prompt and tool table to answer one question. The engine's spawn mapping (`plan/spawn.rs`) now spawns a delegation as `role="reader"` when it reads only: its spec role is `reader`, its accept is not a command, it has no isolation, its tools are within `read` and `grep`, no context URL is `kernel://` or `family://` (a reader's partition refuses both), and no `local://` context names a directory (a reader's partition reads each entry as one file, so `Reader(partition=["local://crates/auth"])` keeps the root child that can list it). Such a child gets its context as its partition, fenced and numbered as its first message, and the prose `Context:` list leaves its brief. Its schema is inlined as the reader's `schema` kwarg: the declared output's schema read through the engine's output resolver, or else the contract's schema item read from the plan's artifacts, which is where Python's `contract(schema(...))` freezes it. The kwarg is the schema's one delivery: the brief drops its own schema lines and JSON-only instruction for a reader that has one. The engine carries that schema from the spawn's start to the spawn mapping in the delegation's transient extras, the way it already carries the brief's lines; nothing of it is journaled. Every other delegation (a Writer, a worktree or container child, a command-accepted reader, one that names a kernel value or a directory) spawns `role="root"` as before. At reap the engine measures context relevance only for a root child: a reader's partition is inlined at spawn, so no fetch row in its transcript could show it was read, and measuring it told the parent its context went unread. The change is in `plan/spawn.rs` (the routing test, the schema lookup, the brief and kwargs edits) `plan/dispatch.rs` (the schema carried from `ensure_spawned`, the routing decided once per spawn against the host's cwd) and `plan/acceptance.rs` (no relevance measure for a routed reader). YI_DESIGN.md section 11's child-spawn paragraph and the plan row of the ARCHITECTURE feature ledger say the same. Kill tests, zero spend. (a) S0's per-role report gains a `plan_reader` row, measured from a Reader declared on a root's own plan tool and spawned by its engine: 802 + 3,869 = 4,671 B in every workspace, the same bytes as the bare reader row and under the 6,000 B kill line. Before this change the same row read 45,561 B bare and 93,453 B in a lane, the leaf root child's prefix. The row is reported, not gated: check_request_budget.py gates the five bare rows it names. (b) A plan Reader with review_pod's schema contract reached `done` with resolution `verified_done` on the first try, through the real finish and verify path, with no fix needed. (d) is paid and runs later in the measure lane (B1-exp-d). The runtime crate grows by 113 lines against this PR's base: 82 of production code (the routing test, the schema lookup and the kwargs and brief edits in `plan/spawn.rs`, 10 lines in `plan/dispatch.rs` and 2 in `plan/acceptance.rs`) and a 31-line unit test inside `plan/spawn.rs` for the declared-output schema, which an integration test cannot reach because the lookup is private to the plan module. The plan estimated 45; the difference is that unit test, the two-source schema lookup and the directory and reap fixes a cold review found. Tests grow by 367 lines: the new `plan_readers.rs` (326: the accepted reader, which also asserts no relevance note reaches the parent, and one-bad-part spawns that cover every clause of the routing test), the `plan_reader` row and its assertion in `request_budget.rs` (33), a resolver and a context file for `judge::a_brief_naming_context_its_wall_denies_is_refused` (7), whose last spawn is a plan Reader and now fetches its context as a partition at spawn, and `node.rs`'s `git_repo` made crate-visible (1) so a worktree-isolated root child has a repository to branch. (D482, D483; Refs #937, Refs #1442) |
| 0.762.0 | 2026-10-25 | This is Bet 2's cell producer (unit B2-cell). `cell_output` takes the recovery dir. The ipython tool passes its context's dir, and a user cell from a client passes none. A cell's joined traceback now goes through `view(Involuntary::Traceback)`. A traceback under the 8,192 B floor reaches the model byte for byte, as before. A recursion traceback the kernel joined at 73,479 B now arrives as the reduced frames with the `RecursionError` line inline and a `[full output: …]` file holding every byte. With no dir, the ceiling's row names the cell that prints the exception again. A `%%bash` cell, or a cell with `!cmd` lines, sends its stdout and stderr through `view(Involuntary::ShellCell)`. The two streams are joined exactly as before, with the `stderr:` label, so a small shell cell reads the same. The command is the cell's shell body, so `%%bash\ncargo test` takes the cargo reducer. When the kernel cut a stream at 65,536 chars, the spill pointer in the cell's notes is handed to the reducer as the cut capture's file. The reducer then reads the whole run from the spill, and the pointer is shown once, at the end of the view, not again among the notes. IPython's `!cmd` does not raise when the command fails, so a `!cmd` cell has no exit signal; it is read as a failing run, which keeps a failing test and its panic inline and costs some bytes on a passing one. Only a `%%bash` cell, which raises `CalledProcessError` on a non-zero exit, takes the passing path when it did not raise. A cell that mixes Python prints and `!cmd` lines is treated as shell output whole, which is the closed list's own wording. A line that opens with `!=`, a continued comparison, is Python and not a shell escape. A Python print in a plain cell is read as printed. (D481; Closes #1485, Refs #1442) |
| 0.761.0 | 2026-10-25 | This PR fixes the plan tool in six units, in commit order: HA-PLAN-arms, HA-PLAN-errors, HA-PLAN-oneparse, CAPS-plan, HA-PLAN-report and HA-PLAN-label. HA-PLAN-arms. A plan row blocked on an `exec://` wait arms a shell command that later runs on the host, outside any sandbox. The plan tool's `arms` read only a top-level `on`, so the row form its own schema documents, `todos[].on`, armed its command with no gate, and `plan.op` from a cell armed with no gate at all. `schedule::clock::armed_commands` now walks every object of a call for an `on` and returns each command it arms. The tool and `plan.op` share one reading, `plan::request::armed`, over the args as sent and as the whole-plan reading mends a string of rows, each command once. `plan.op` judges each command through the gate a heartbeat's `exec://` source meets, the one `rlm_heartbeat.create` uses, and refuses with the code `denied`. HA-PLAN-errors. The schema shows three ops, `set`, `view` and `accepted_by_user`, while an unknown or missing op listed sixteen and the hints named `init`, `start`, `op=block`, `append`, `decompose` and `op=accept`. The legal-op list is now the schema's own `SHOWN_OPS`, and the no-plan, open-plan, illegal-step, misplaced-`on`, depth and checker-timeout hints say the move as a `set` row or `accepted_by_user`. Hidden ops still parse on every surface; only the teaching changed. A cell's `plan.op` and `yi plan` read no set row's block or fail, so their refusals respell those three hints as the op that does it there (`op block`, `op fail`); a test follows each hint from a cell, and one from the shell. A drop on a running todo still reads "ends with fail and a cause", since a dogfood fixture pins those words, and now adds the set row that does it. Each wall names what the model can do: a plan or journal record over its cap says to send fewer or shorter rows (a test sends a change past the plan cap, then the same change with fewer rows, which lands), a checkpoint changed behind the engine says to delete it so the next op rebuilds it from the journal (a test runs that remedy), and a workspace past the hash cap says to run the check by hand and close the todo with `accepted_by_user`. `PlanOpError`'s code, census kind and journaled flag were three matches over the same 52 variants; they are one table now, in kinds.rs, which takes 78 lines out of the plan engine. HA-PLAN-oneparse. The engine's `set` keeps only the rows it names and reads a struck row as pending. The tool hid that with its own reading: a JSON-string todos list is read as the list, a row the set does not name is carried as it stands, and a struck row is dropped once the rest lands. `plan.op` and `yi plan` (with the console's submit, through `authority::submit`) called the parser and the reducer directly, so a cell's or a shell's set deleted every row it left out, reopened a struck row, and refused a string todos list as missing. Every surface now goes through one `apply::apply_set`, and `tool::request` reads a string todos list itself. The notes the tool prints ("2 rows not named stay as they are", "y was struck, so it was dropped") are notices on `plan.op` and the CLI. The reducer is untouched, so old journals replay the same. A struck row's drop carries no request id or revision of its own, since the set it follows already moved the plan. CAPS-plan. Four cuts in what the plan shows the model said nothing at the cut. A child's failure cause and a refused contract's item tail were cut to `CAUSE_CHARS` (500) chars with no mark; past it the cause now keeps its first 500 chars, then a row `[… 500 of N chars (CAUSE_CHARS 500); the rest: read plan://<plan>/artifacts/<hex>]`, and the whole text is a plan artifact at that address, which `read` opens. The view's window notice said "N more todos not shown"; it is now the same row, naming the shown count over all todos, the `WINDOW` (8) cap and the call `plan {"op": "view", "full": true}`. `frontier_text` and the compaction directive listed every row; each now lists `FRONTIER` (16) and names the rest the same way, and the frontier's cut takes ready rows before the running and blocked ones. Each row is written by yi-tools' `cut`, the one writer of the row. A rendered outcome printed `dispatched:` for every newly admitted row, the owner's own included; it now names only rows a child takes. Juror reasons are left to the approved judge.rs deletion. HA-PLAN-report. The plan report set a todo's resolution from the last record that named the todo with a `to` state, so an edge added to a done todo, which records `to: done` again, reset the resolution to none. Only a record that moves the state now ends the todo anew; the time buckets read the same. `yi why <plan>/<todo>` found commits with a fixed-string grep for `Plan: plan://<plan>/<todo>`, which `p/todo-2`'s trailer also contains; it now asks git for the trailer as a whole line, as a basic regexp named on the command line so a user's `grep.patternType` never reads it, and a todo lists only its own commits. Its git log read cuts at `CAP` (64 KiB), keeping the head and the tail, and said nothing; past it the reply ends with `[… K of T bytes of git log (CAP 65536); the rest: git log --format='%H %s' --basic-regexp --grep '<the same pattern>']`, in the text and as `cut` in `--json`. `yi why` has no narrower form of a todo's reverse query, so the row names the git call it runs, which no cap cuts. HA-PLAN-label. `Todo::from_text` cut a long text to its first 80 chars, so two todos that shared that head got one label, and the todo store and the plan validator refused the second as a duplicate. A text past the cap is now labelled by its head and the first 8 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the whole text, within the 80 chars, and the text stays whole in the note. The same text always gives the same label, and no stored label is recomputed: a list written by an older build keeps its labels, which still read as cut. The todo tool named an item by running the name through `from_text`, which would now give a copied label and its ellipsis a digest of their own. It now reads a whole text past the cap as that text's own label, so each of two todos sharing a head is named by its whole text; it cuts a copied label or an id-led line plainly, and its lookup matches a cut item by the text it was cut from as well as by its label. A whole text retyped in another case misses that digest, so the lookup then reads the digest label's head as a prefix, as a plain cut did. The head alone names a cut item only while no other todo shares it. A rendered list sent back through the todo tool's `set` reads each `…` row as the label it shows, so the row keeps its label and the note, intent and ask its label carries. The todo replay goldens keep main's text; their compare spells each long row's label as today's code does before it reads the rest word for word, so every recorded call still replays. A `local://` address could not name a path with a space, since a raw space is refused and `%20` was kept literally; in a `local://` path `%20` now decodes to a space and prints back as `%20`, and a `deny_url` prefix written with a raw space walls that spelling too. Only `%20` decodes, so a file whose name holds a literal `%20` has no spelling. The yi-types golden fixtures and schemas.lock are unchanged. (D480; Closes #1478, Refs #1442) |
| 0.760.0 | 2026-10-25 | This is Bet 2's loud-caps part of Yi's next shape, one bundle of two units: CAPS-context-tools and OUT-read-grep-bytes. CAPS-context-tools. `yi_tools::cut_row` is public (it came with CAPS-runtime), so a crate that holds a cap builds the same `[… kept of total what (CAP limit); the rest: next]` row the reducer does. A context source over its byte budget ends in that row, naming the budget, instead of `[... truncated: N bytes over budget ...]`. `fit` takes the call that gets the rest from its caller: `read AGENTS.md in full` for an instruction file, `read("yi://skills/<name>") for one skill` for the catalog's fallback, and `await memory.search("words")` for the memory block. The compaction view says how many outstanding errors the 12-row cap dropped. A malformed edit header echoes at most 120 chars of the line and says how many it held. A default `read` window cut at 2,000 lines names `READ_LINE_CAP`, and `get_context` says its skeleton scan stopped at 2,000 source files. The 24-range `EARLIER_CAP` and the 160-char brief cuts in the same view are left alone: the earlier ranges are carried from one compaction to the next, so a row there needs a count the persisted view does not hold. OUT-read-grep-bytes. An unpaged `read local://<file>` keeps its first 51,200 bytes on a char boundary and ends in the row a history listing gets, naming kept of total, the cap and the offset that reads the rest. A window the model asked for (`offset` or `limit`) is never cut. A grep page stops between hits once its rows hold `PAGE_BYTES` (51,200), counting each row's path and line number, and its continuation row names the cap that cut the page (`PAGE_BYTES`, `PAGE_CAP` or `BLOCK_PAGE_CAP`) beside the offset. One hit's window may pass the cap, so a page always advances, and a block hit larger than the cap is still returned whole. (D479; Closes #1477, Refs #44, Refs #1442) |
| 0.759.0 | 2026-10-25 | A console chat pane's orb and question card now draw on one of a hundred kitty id pairs the pane leases (E-uilife-kitty-ids). The ids were `7800 + 2 x pane` for the orb and `7200 + 2 x pane` for the card, where the pane id counts every pane the console ever opened and is never reused, so the hundredth pane's orb transmitted on 8001, an id the avatar pool had already given a pane's close button, and its close deleted 8000 and 8001 under the gear and that button; at pane 250 a close deleted the notebook's 7701. The per-frame map of which pane drew which chat's images now also holds the pane's slot: a pane that first draws a chat takes the lowest slot no other entry holds, and the entry's removal, when the pane closes, is rebound or parks its chat, deletes the pair and frees the slot. Slots run 0 to 99, so orbs stay in 7800-7999 and cards in 7200-7399. A pane past the hundredth live one holds no slot, draws its mark as text and says so once in its transcript, `[…] 100 of 100 image slots in use · this pane's mark is text until a pane closes`, and takes the first slot a close frees. A chat brought back from the parked set starts fresh image state, so it never re-places an id deleted when it left. The lease runs before the frame is drawn, so a new pane's first frame already knows whether it draws an image. The whole id map, from the cards' 7200 to the browser frames' 0xF00000, is one module comment in app/ids.rs, and the browser's comment points there. The console notebook now keeps image data for a session's newest cell with images only (E-uilife-notebook-mem). A cell kept up to four PNGs of up to 512 KiB of base64 and a session kept fifty cells, never evicted while the console ran, so a plotting loop of 200 KB figures held about 13 MB per session in the notebook alone though only one image was ever drawn and the rest were listed by size. A cell now records each kept image's base64 length, which its `▲ image · N KB png` rows read, and the cell an update gives images clears the data of every other cell of the session; the cap rows for dropped and older images are unchanged. The kitty placement read the first kept image of that cell while its comment said the newest; it now places the newest. The chat port's replayed and committed entries carry the same images and stay whole on purpose, since `/tree` and the cell-to-entry match read every entry; a comment there now says so. The recorded fixture is a real kernel cell that writes a 203,188-byte PNG with the standard library and hands it to `attach_image`'s emitter, scrubbed by `evals/record.py --scrub-only` with the image's base64 put back, since the scrubber masks it. Units E-uilife-kitty-ids and E-uilife-notebook-mem. (D478; Closes #1494, Closes #806, Refs #1442) |
| 0.758.0 | 2026-10-25 | Four crates each carried their own base64 encoder: the console's OSC 52 copy string, the orb's kitty transmit payload, ACP's terminal output chunks, and the URL-safe unpadded form oauth uses for the PKCE S256 challenge. They now share crates/types/src/encoding.rs, which holds `base64` (standard, padded) and `base64url_nopad` (URL-safe, unpadded) over one loop, pure and dependency-free like `image::is_strict_base64` beside it. yi-orb was a leaf with no internal dependencies, so it now depends on yi-types; scripts/guardrails/boundaries.toml, the crate table in docs/YI_DESIGN.md section 2 and the crate map in docs/ARCHITECTURE.md name the new edge. The orb's test-side decoder in crates/orb/tests/kitty.rs stays, since an independent check of the encoder is its point. ACP's own padding test went with its copy, because the yi-types test now covers every tail length against Python's base64 module and the RFC 7636 appendix B challenge. The console OSC 52 test and the orb kitty tests pass unchanged and fail under a swapped-alphabet encoder, so they pin the bytes; the ACP chunk test builds its expectation with the same encoder and pins only the wiring. A new yi-oauth test pins that `pkce::generate` emits the RFC 7636 S256 form, 43 unpadded base64url symbols. The encoder is `#[inline]` so the orb's per-frame call compiles at yi-orb's opt-level 3 rather than yi-types' dist `z`. (D477; Closes #1505, Refs #1442) |
| 0.757.0 | 2026-10-25 | A child's card sits under the kernel cell that was running when the host admitted it, by record and not by timing. The spawn line a parent journals on its own transcript (`ChildSpawned`) gains an optional `spawnedBy`, the tool call id of the cell that ran `rlm.run`; the field is additive, so an older line decodes with it absent. `KernelBridge::execute_cell` takes the call's id, the kernel service holds it while the cell runs (the loop runs an exec call alone, so one cell runs at a time), and the subagent host reads it when it admits a child, writing it to the trail line and to the roster's `ChildView`. The TUI adopts a child under the cell its record names; before, it took whichever `ipython` cell was running when the roster first listed the child, so a child the first cell spawned, first seen while the second ran, waited on the second and committed under it. The record names the cell running at admission, not the cell that asked: a spawn from outside any cell (the plan timer's dispatch backstop runs off the loop's turns) records the model's cell if one is running then, and none otherwise. A record that names no cell (a spawn outside any cell) places the card under none, so it waits on no unrelated cell; only an update with no record at all (the console's forwarded updates) still uses the timing guess, which is no worse than before. A child's completion mail stays quiet after its card is dropped by a reset or the cap, as it did while every card lived forever. A committed card releases its cached answer rows, since only a live card draws from them. `crates/runtime/src/kernel.rs` grows by two lines (1,188 to 1,190) for the slot. Child cards now end with the transcript they belong to and stop piling up. A `/new` or a rewind cleared the history and the live region but kept every card, so `/agents`, the alt-arrow focus cycle and the status count went on listing the children of an earlier transcript, and each card's feed held its child's whole session alive. `reset_transcript` moves from `app.rs` to `app/children.rs` and now also drops the cards, the order, the committed set and the focus; the dropped ids are remembered, so the next roster tick brings back a child that is still running and leaves a finished one in the old transcript. Finished cards past 32 leave oldest first once they are committed to scrollback, never a running card and never the focused one, and `/agents` then ends with `[…] agents: 32 of 33 finished shown · cap 32 finished children · older cards are in the transcript`. `commit_finished_tasks`, which runs on every child update, cloned the whole order and every card (up to 16 KB of answer each) before it looked at a status; it now checks each card by reference and clones only the one it commits. `app.rs` goes from 1,098 lines to 1,095. A running child card renders its answer once per change, not once per frame. Each frame cloned every running card (up to 16 KB of answer) and ran markdown over the whole answer to keep its last four rows, and every child delta asked for a frame, so K streaming children cost K full renders a frame. `TaskState` now keeps the card's answer rows (`AnswerRows`), reused while the answer text, the width and the theme are unchanged, and `TaskCell::lines_at` takes the running time as an argument instead of a cloned card; the title, the step rows and the fade are still drawn each frame. The rows are reused only for the same text, not the same length: a card at its 16 KiB cap keeps its length while the text moves. Measured with eight running cards holding 16 KiB answers at width 100 over 200 frames: 31.2 ms a frame in debug and 12.4 ms in dist before, 0.50 ms in debug after. The rows are checked against the uncached renderer's output recorded at the head before the change (`crates/tui/tests/fixtures/task-card-rows.json`). Two members of this bundle were killed by their zero-spend tests and are not built. E-uilife-history would have dropped the `details` of tool cells older than the resize replay cap. Its test replayed the five recorded sessions under `crates/tui/tests/fixtures/sessions` (40 tool cells), summed each cell's retained strings and values (its arguments, result text and `details`), and projected 10,000 cells at the 95th-percentile cell, 16,981 B: 161.9 MiB, under the 256 MiB bar, so a session of that length holds less than a browser tab and the cut is not worth its risk. E-uiperf-delta would have appended a streamed delta to the live strings instead of rebuilding them from the whole message. Its test fed a 64 KiB answer as 3,276 deltas of 20 B in a debug build: the rebuild inside `arrive` cost 2.4 ms in all, under the 50 ms bar, while the whole delta path cost about 90 ms, nearly all of it the linear commit scan the unit does not touch; a build with the append measured no gain at 64 KiB. Members: HB-child-spawned-by, E-uilife-children, E-uiperf-card, E-uilife-history (killed), E-uiperf-delta (killed). (Closes #1482, Refs #1442) |
| 0.756.0 | 2026-10-25 | The next-step hint engine keeps its eleven edges and loses what nothing reads (HD-D08-affordance-trim). `Edge.pitfalls` is gone, with its two size caps and the `pitfalls_size` check: no renderer ever printed a pitfall and graph.json carries none. The relation names `instead`, `before` and `after_refusal` are gone: graph.json uses `then` and `after_error` only, and the renderer never reads the relation. Five closed predicates that no host asserts and no edge uses are gone: `output_capped`, `todo_open`, `plan_ready_nonempty`, `worktree_unmerged` and `inbox_nonempty`. A graph or a refiner proposal that names any of them now fails to parse, in Rust and in evals/graph/refine.py, which mirrors the table. The shared fixture evals/fixtures/graph/structural.json drops the five conditions and the pitfalls and the removed relation names from its valid cases. Its two pitfall-cap cases become a parse refusal for an edge that carries pitfalls and one for a removed relation, and it gains a case for a removed predicate. Not cut here: `done_refused`, `blocked_on(user)`, `blocked_on(child)`, `blocked_on(external)` and the `child_state` arguments `queued`, `failed`, `needs_you`, `stuck` and `repossession_pending` are also asserted at no seam and cited by no edge today. They stay because the haul finding behind this unit named the five above and no unit in this stack asserts them; trimming them is a follow-up that also moves the `valid` case in the shared fixture off `done_refused`. The rendered hint text does not change by a byte: the golden strings in crates/runtime/tests/affordance.rs, which the old producers rendered before the graph existed, pass untouched. `Graph::check` stays as the shipped graph's validity gate, and the offline refiner (#686) keeps scoring edge patches against it. The graph test that listed seven of the nine tool error kinds now takes the list from `ToolErrorKind` itself. The match that walks the kinds has no wildcard arm, so a tenth kind fails to compile that test file until it has an arm. It is walked, and held equal to the arguments `result_error` admits, only once the arm before it names it as the next kind: an arm that ends the chain early (`Timeout => None`) compiles and is not caught, because stable Rust cannot list an enum's variants and a list kept beside the enum can be forgotten the same way. The predicate table keeps its list of nine kinds: it is the closed vocabulary the Python mirror and the shared fixture are compared against, and the test is what keeps it equal to the enum. D219's row in docs/ARCHITECTURE.md still names pitfalls and their 3 x 120 byte cap; this change drops both. The row is named here and not rewritten, because decision rows are numbered by the recorder (#975). The next-step paragraph of section 6 in docs/YI_DESIGN.md is corrected to match. (D476; Refs #686, Refs #1442) |
| 0.755.0 | 2026-10-25 | `rlm.merge_worktree` takes an optional `check`, a shell command run in the child's lane before its work is merged into this checkout. A parent that wanted to test a child's work before landing it had no way to: the lane path is not in any reply, and after the hand-back the lane is gone. The check meets the same host gate a spawn's `check` meets (the user's gate rules, the wall's command-text check and walled roots, then the broker's decision on the bash call it amounts to), and a refused one never runs: the call answers `the check is refused before it runs on the host: ...` and the lane stays held, so a merge without the check still works. An allowed check runs in the lane with a cleared environment (`PATH`, `HOME`, `LANG`, `TMPDIR`) under the session deadline, or ten minutes when the session has none, off the kernel's executor like the rest of the hand-back. A check that is not a string is refused, as `rlm.run` refuses one, and a blank one is no check. A green check proceeds to the merge as before. A red or cut one settles the lane and keeps its branch, the outcome a conflict already gives, so this is not a third hand-back road and no disposition is recorded: the reply is `merged: false` with the `files` it changed, `check: {exit, tail}` and a `next` sentence naming the kept branch with the `git merge` that lands it anyway and the `git branch -D` that drops it. A child that changed nothing has its branch deleted at release, and its `next` says no branch was kept. The check runs before the settle because the settle releases the lane, so the files it writes outside the ignored paths are committed with the child's work. `tail` is the check's output through the view the goal gate uses (`check_view`): whole under `VIEW_CEILING` (16,384 B), past it the head, one row naming kept over total and the cap, and the end, and a capture past its 30,000 bytes a stream adds the capture's own `[capture cut: ...]` row. `exit` is null when the check was cut, and the tail then opens with a `[… cut at the session deadline; ...]` row (or the 600000 ms check timeout with no deadline). Each row's remedy is one bash call that runs the check on the candidate commit in a scratch worktree and removes it, so it leaves this checkout's branch alone and works whether or not the branch was kept. The Python `rlm.merge_worktree` gains the `check` keyword. Section 14 of the design names the check; the lanes ledger row names its journeys. (D475; Closes #1488, Refs #1442) |
| 0.754.0 | 2026-10-25 | The unit in this change is E-cli-ask-output. Plain `yi ask` printed each text delta as it arrived and one newline at the very end, so a run of two turns that each said something printed `Let me look.Done.` and a script could not tell the narration from the answer. A turn that said text now ends with a blank line before the next turn's text is printed. The blank line is written when that next text arrives, so a turn that said nothing between two that did adds none, and the run still ends with the single newline it always had. `--schema` runs print nothing as they go and are unchanged. `--json` keeps printing every event. A new `--no-deltas` flag, a row in the command table, drops the `message_update` lines from that stream: on the faux provider `yi ask --json hi` prints 2,250 bytes and 1,945 with the flag, and on a real model the dropped lines are one per token. Every other event is kept and the stream still ends in `agent_end`. The flag is read by `ask` alone and only with `--json`: `yi ask --no-deltas` without `--json`, `yi stats --json --no-deltas` and a bare `yi --json --no-deltas`, which opens the console, all exit 2 with ``error: --no-deltas needs `yi ask --json` ``, so neither is a flag that silently matched nothing, while `yi --json --no-deltas <words>`, which asks, keeps the flag. The bash, zsh and fish completions name it, and the drift test against `yi --help` holds them to it. The Terminal-Bench adapter's `grep -v message_update` is left alone, since it may run an older binary. docs/YI_DESIGN.md section 17.1's `ask` row states both behaviours. (Closes #1481, Refs #1442) |
| 0.753.0 | 2026-10-25 | This is Bet 2's loud-caps part for the runtime's own lists (unit CAPS-runtime). Seven cuts that shrank what the model reads without saying so now end in the row the reducer already uses, `[… kept of total what (CAP limit); the rest: next]`. One function builds that row in both crates: yi-tools' `cut`, made public here as `cut_row` (its longer name keeps rustfmt from folding the signature), which the runtime imports. The context-budget change replaces it with a public `Cut` struct and has to move these runtime callers with it. `rlm.find_models(query, limit)` returned the first `limit` matches and nothing else. The host reply now carries a `cut` row when more matched, naming the matches it held back and the call that lists them all (`await rlm.find_models("", limit=31)`). The `rlm` shim in `python/yi_runtime` reads only `models`, so a cell does not print the row yet. That edit moves the runtime identity hash, so it ships with the one Python release; #1487 stays open until the shim prints `cut`, and this change only Refs it. The `children:` environment row named every member of every state. It names at most eight a state now, keeps each state's true count, and ends in a row counting the names left out and pointing at `await rlm.status()`. The cap is per state so that a hundred finished children cannot hide the one that needs the parent. A todo error that lists rows (a wrong label, an ambiguous prefix, several running, a wrong phase) lists at most twelve and ends in a row naming `LIST_ROWS` and `todo {"op": "view"}`. A 40-row list was about 4 KB on every failed call. An open-children list and the evidence echo are left alone. A refused `answer` echoed its whole draft a second time as numbered rows. It echoes the rows that fit in `ANSWER_ECHO_BYTES` (4,096) now, then a row giving the lines shown over the lines held, the held file and the offset to read from. The file still holds the whole draft, so an `edit` of a line past the cut starts with a `read` at that offset. The memory block's `+N not loaded` index row is a cut row now: notes loaded over notes indexed, the cap that cut (`LOAD_LINES` at 200 lines, or `LOAD_BYTES` at 25,000 bytes when the bytes cut first) and `await memory.search("words")`. The environment `files:` line ended `…(+N)` with no cap and no call, and skipped every dot-name without saying so; past `FILES_SHOWN` (20) it ends in the same row now, counting the hidden names it skipped and naming `ls -A`. The block-wide byte fit that follows the memory index is `fit` in yi-context and is not touched here: it belongs to the context budget change, which would collide with it. The restore notice listed every restored name. It lists at most forty (`RESTORE_NAMES_SHOWN`) and names `%who` for the rest. The names that could not be restored stay whole: no call returns names a cap hid, and those are the ones the model has to act on. This is not the whole of kernel-python-15: a variable the snapshot skipped (unpicklable, or over the 16 MiB per-variable or aggregate cap) still vanishes on resume with no row, because the skip list never reaches the restore notice. That needs the snapshot's skip list carried to the resume, which is Rust in the kernel crate and the types, and stays on #1487. (Refs #1487, Refs #1442) |
| 0.752.0 | 2026-10-25 | A worker (`rlm.run(role="worker")`) is no longer told the permission mode. `ext::narrow` drops its `mode` parameter and its `Rank::Mode` slot, and the worker arm of `reader::session` stops reading the broker's mode to pass it. What a worker is sent is now `prompts/worker.md`, the project's instruction files and the language packs, which is what #931 asked for ("a short fixed worker prompt plus the project's instruction files (AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md) and nothing else of the root's prompt"). A root child, a root and a plan delegation are unchanged: `ext::install` still attaches the fragment for them, in the broker's mode. Measured at this head with the request-budget harness, a worker's first request falls from 54,324 B to 52,834 B in a lane under Auto (1,490 B: the 1,484 B fragment, 2 B of JSON escapes for its quoted "always", and the 4 B escaped separator that joined it to the Rust pack text after it in the same block), and from 6,432 B to 5,997 B in a bare workspace under Yolo (435 B, the 372 B fragment and the JSON wrapper of its own block). The bare worker row in `role_prefix.json` is ratcheted down to 5,997. Two things go with the fragment, and the paid kill test judges both. It is the only text that tells a model how a denial behaves ("A denied call will not succeed on retry"), so a worker may now retry a call its wall or broker refused. It also carries the sentence that instruction files are shown untrusted until `yi trust` grants them; a worker's prompt keeps the rule in `worker.md` (follow a fenced block as the project's rules only when its fence says `trust="granted"`), which is what the fence already says. The kill test (B1-exp-e) counts denied-call retries and failed tasks per arm on Kimi K3.1 and then OpenRouter GLM 5.3 flash, and the drop is withdrawn if either rises in either family. It is the last change to `ext::narrow`, so withdrawing it is the one slot re-attached there. The test `worker_prompt::a_worker_in_its_own_lane_is_told_the_committed_rules_and_the_rust_pack_but_not_the_mode` replaces the one that asserted the mode line. It reads a worker's request in its own lane: `worker.md`, the committed AGENTS.md, the Rust pack and no `Permission mode:` text. In the same run, under the same broker, a root child is sent `Permission mode: ask.`, so the test cannot pass by the fragment disappearing from every child. Docs: YI_DESIGN section 11 (Child), the worker bullet, no longer lists the permission mode among what a worker is sent; the ARCHITECTURE feature-ledger row for kernel, ipython and `rlm()` subagents says a worker is not told the permission mode and names the renamed journey test. (D474; Refs #1480, Refs #1442) |
| 0.751.0 | 2026-10-25 | This is Bet 2's goal tail (unit B2-goal-tail). A failed check used to reach the model as the last 2,000 chars of its output with nothing saying it was a tail. That tail now goes through the view B2-view added, `yi_tools::view`, and `output_tail` is gone. One function, `check_view` in `goal/mod.rs`, takes the check's command, the directory it ran in and its capture, and returns the view's text. Past `VIEW_CEILING` (16,384 B) the text is the head, one row and the tail, and the row names kept over total, the cap and the rerun that writes the rest to a file: `bash (cd '<dir>' && <command>) > view.log 2>&1, then read view.log`. The `cd` is the point: a check that ran in a child's lane or a `cwd_subdir` is read by a parent whose bash is rooted elsewhere, and a bare command would rerun in the wrong tree and could pass. Output under the ceiling now arrives whole where it used to be cut at 2,000 chars. Three places print a failed check, and all three call `check_view`: the goal gate (`run_check`, which the gate, the discovery drain, a child's result check, the child finish hold-back and the discovery router all call), a checker item's refused detail (`verdict_of` in `plan/verify.rs`) and the `exec://` source of a heartbeat (`schedule/adapter.rs`). The pass evidence of `run_cmd_said` keeps its own 300-char tail and its own loud row, now taken from the raw streams. A refused checker item cannot name a rerun, because the tree it ran in is a materialized snapshot or a verification lane removed before the author reads the refusal. It passes the plan's new `Artifacts::spill_dir` as the view's spill directory instead, so the reducer foreground bash uses can run on it and the cut row reads `read <file>`; the file holds the capture, redacted, and lives and dies with the plan. A real `pytest -v` run now reads as the reducer's view (the head, the FAILED row, the verdict, the passing tests counted and omitted) plus a `[full output: <file>]` pointer, where the goal gate, which has no spill directory, shows the middle-cut view. A check's capture is cut at `OUTPUT_CAP` (30,000 B a stream) before the view sees it, so the view's total can be smaller than what the check printed. `check_view` appends the capture's own cut row after the view's, and both capture sites name `OUTPUT_CAP` in place of the literal, so that row quotes the real cap. A 32,955 B `pytest -v` run through the goal gate reads `[… 16074 of 30038 bytes kept (VIEW_CEILING 16384); the rest: bash (cd '…' && …) > view.log 2>&1, then read view.log]` and `[capture cut: kept 30000 of 32955 bytes (OUTPUT_CAP 30000 per stream); no file kept the rest]`. The view is printed once whole, in the refusal, and two later copies of the same evidence shrank to what a reminder needs. The goal's stored `check_failure`, which every continuation prints, and the note of a todo blocked by the refusal cap, which every frontier prints, keep the first line and the last 2,000 B with a row naming `REPEAT_BYTES` and where the whole went. Rows of a discovery drain or of a child's result that name one red task print its evidence once and say so on the later rows. Without this the change would have multiplied a 2 KB tail by eight in each of those places. The `exec://` source needed one more guard. A channel refuses a message past `MESSAGE_MAX_BYTES` (16,384 B) whole, with its data set to null, so a red level with a ceiling-sized view in it would stop matching an `ok=false` filter and the todo that filter makes would never be created. When the level and its output would serialize past the cap, the message keeps `ok` and `exit`, a row naming the cap and the rerun, and the end of the output, where the verdict and the capture's cut row are; it drops the head until the message fits, counting each byte as JSON sends it (a quote or a newline is two bytes there). A `ceiling` argument on `view`, set from the channel's cap, would keep more of the view's own shape here, and belongs to B2-view's owner. The behavior fixture `done-claim-red-check-refused` is unchanged: its check is `false`, which prints nothing, and its assertions name only `completion rejected` and the exit line. (Closes #1475, Refs #1442) |
| 0.750.0 | 2026-10-25 | This PR carries two units of Yi's next shape, E-ci-live-weekly and HD-G02-surface-trims. The paid weekly live job, `live-weekly`, moves out of `.forgejo/workflows/tracking-hygiene.yml` into its own `.forgejo/workflows/live-weekly.yml`. The job is the three flash runs, the Anthropic run and the OpenAI run, with its 40 minute timeout, rust-cache key and per-secret skips unchanged. The new workflow keeps the weekly Monday 05:17 UTC trigger the job already ran on and adds `workflow_dispatch`, which tracking-hygiene already had; the trigger now exists in each file for its own jobs only, so the paid job runs once a week. tracking-hygiene.yml keeps its one `tracking` job, whose milestone, hygiene and catalog-drift steps spend no provider money, and its header about milestone dates. The one-routing-pin check in `evals/selftest.py` (the ledger judges a PR's live run by main's runs of the same routing) now reads `live-weekly.yml` where it read tracking-hygiene.yml, since the `EVAL_ROUTING` pin moved with the job. docs/FORGE.md lists the new file and names it as the reader of the three provider keys. A PR whose only change to the tool surface removes words no longer owes a Claims ledger. `scripts/guardrails/baselines/tool_surface.json` now keeps each key's text beside its sha256, and `surface_diff` in `scripts/pr_body.py` splits a changed key into `reworded` and `trimmed`: a key is trimmed when its byte count fell and its sentences, with whitespace collapsed, are a subsequence of the old text's sentences. A cut inside a sentence is a rewrite, because dropping `not` from `a cd does not carry over` is shorter, is made of the old words and says the opposite. Only a key that grew or was reworded owes the ledger, and an added key still owes the neighbour matrix and the dogfood table. `check_request_budget.py` still reports a trimmed key as moved, because the lock has to be refreshed by `--update`; it only stops telling the author that the ledger is owed for it. The first lock written in this shape is its own Ratchet commit and raises nothing, and a base lock that still holds bare hashes compares by hash and cannot show a cut, so the first trim after the format change owes the ledger as before. The fixtures are `identity.md` before and after the autofixer's trim in 2b77affff, whose hashes are the lock's values either side of that commit. The lock keeps the text where the plan sketched a byte count beside the hash, because a hash and a byte count cannot say whether new text is made of the old text's sentences, and every tool description is one long line, so a line-level subset check would never fire. docs/YI_DESIGN.md section 7.1 and the guardrails table in section 21 say what the lock holds. Units in this PR: E-ci-live-weekly and HD-G02-surface-trims. (D473; Closes #1466, Refs #1442) |
| 0.749.0 | 2026-10-25 | This is unit B2-jobs of Bet 2. A finished background job reached the model raw. Its `<async_result>` carried every byte of the capture, up to 30,000 B a stream, and reclaim never cuts that text. A poll of the job carried the same raw bytes. `JobReport::view` now runs the capture through `yi_tools::view` with the `Job` producer, and both the poll and the announcement print it, so the two bodies are byte for byte the same and the no-reannounce rule is untouched. A job keeps the recovery dir its call ran with, so a lossy view can point at a file. `JobReport::output` stays whole: a program's polled value and the kernel's job reply read it, not the view. A cut capture used to print two notices for one cut: the `[capture cut: kept K of T bytes …; every byte: <file>]` row and then the `[full output: <file>]` pointer (#1048 item 5). It now prints one row, `[full output, kept K of T bytes (OUTPUT_CAP 30000 per stream), every byte: <file>]`. That row is the pointer the reducer is handed, so the view counts it against the ceiling and the selector still finds the file in it. A cut with no file still says `[capture cut: …; no file kept the rest]`. The program value's `cut` field carries the same one row. A red `cargo test` of 2,000 cases, recorded for this unit from a real scratch crate (64,656 B), now reaches the model as its failing test, the `left`/`right` lines and the summary inside 16,384 B. A 258,672 B build whose one error is its last line keeps that error inline and names the one file holding every byte. The kill test is B2-view's corpus, which this unit does not change. (Closes #1474, Refs #1048, Refs #1442) |
| 0.748.0 | 2026-10-25 | HB-utf8-rest: yi-ai, yi-context, yi-kernel and yi-mcp-cli now deny `clippy::string_slice` at their crate roots, so no byte-index slice of a `str` compiles under clippy in those crates. The fifteen slices they held were boundary-safe, each behind a `find` of an ASCII byte or a hand-written walk back to a char boundary; they are now `split_once`, `str::get`, or `floor_char_boundary` and `ceil_char_boundary`, which deletes the five hand-written walks. The pending list keeps only runtime and tools, which other changes clear. HA-INT-abort: an abort now cuts the provider request as well as the loop's reading of it. `InterruptSignal::fire` sets the cut flag the HTTP adapters already read between SSE events, so the pump stops at its next event and drops the connection instead of letting the provider generate, and bill, to `max_tokens`; on Kimi's recorded 91-event bash stream an abort at the third event now reads three events, where it read all 91. The cut flag is now per request: opening a request installs a fresh flag and leaves the old one set, so the next prompt cannot un-cut a pump still blocked on its read. An aborted OpenRouter turn now takes the cut path's settlement, so its cost is booked from the generation record. A turn cut at the reasoning budget waits up to 45 s for the provider's settled `Done`; an abort during that wait now ends it at once and is recorded as an aborted turn, with no length redrive. YI_DESIGN.md §4.5 says both: a fire sets the request's cut, and each request opens with a fresh one. Two races in the signal are closed: `wait` creates its `Notified` before it checks `fired`, so a fire between the check and the await is heard, and `fire` moves the epoch before it sets `fired` while `reset_if_epoch` re-reads the epoch after clearing and restores `fired` when a fire raced it; a three-thread stress of 100,000 rounds lost about 10,000 fires before (8,370 to 13,197 across runs) and loses none now. Cursor's stop function now reads the cut flag alone, which a fire sets, so the signal no longer hands out `fired` and its `Default` is derived. HA-PLAN-leak: GLM's argument markup is repaired once, in yi-ai's tool-call decoder, for every tool. When GLM's call channel leaks `<arg_key>` and `<arg_value>` into a call's arguments, as in `{"done<arg_key>evidence": …}` or `{"done": "evidence</arg_key><arg_value>…"}`, the OpenAI-compatible, Responses and Anthropic mappers now read the field back before any tool sees the call, and keep the stray word in front of the markup as a `true` flag, which the plan tool reads as the op; the todo tool infers the op from the repaired field. Before, only the todo and plan tools repaired it, each with its own copy, and every other tool received the leaked key as a field; those two copies are deleted. A login profile's `api_base`, the endpoint every Kimi request, catalog refresh and usage read sends the account's bearer token to, is now held to the rule the profile's other endpoints already follow: https, or http to an exact loopback host. A profile naming a plain-http remote base is refused when it is read, where before the token went out in cleartext. (D472; Closes #1469, Closes #138, Closes #139, Closes #140, Closes #141, Refs #1442) |
| 0.747.0 | 2026-10-25 | The units in this change are E-cli-tidy, E-cli-table and E-cli-refuse. E-cli-tidy moves the shared argument parser (`Args`, `Resume`, `parse_args` and its two value parsers) out of crates/cli/src/main.rs into crates/cli/src/args.rs, and turns code that was repeated into one function each. The runtime builder that printed `error:` and exited 1 was written out six times, the session repository for the run's directory five times, the one-line JSON printer six times, the `--schema` load twice and the pinned `laya[serve]` package twice. Two behaviours change. `yi serve` now returns exit 1 when it cannot build its runtime, instead of calling `process::exit` inside a function that returns an exit code. The resume hint and the lane line printed at quit are dimmed only when stderr is a terminal, so a script that captures stderr gets plain text with no escape bytes. E-cli-table adds crates/cli/src/commands.rs, one table of the 28 verbs (name, the function dispatch calls, the words it takes, a one-line summary) and the 32 flags (name, value hint, summary). `dispatch` looks the first word up in the table instead of a 28-arm match, so the help text and the routing cannot disagree, and the parser refuses a flag the table does not hold, so neither can the help text and the parser. The six verbs answered before the shared flags parse (update, plugin, login, logout, setup, mcp) are rows too; reached only when another word came first, they now exit 2 asking for the verb first, where the words used to go to the model. `yi --help`, `yi -h` and a bare `yi help` print both tables and exit 0; `yi help me debug` is still a prompt. The completion files under completions/ named 10 of the 28 verbs and 16 of the 32 flags; they now name all of them, and a test fails when they drift from `yi --help`. docs/YI_DESIGN.md section 17.1 loses its "there is no `--help`" line. E-cli-refuse adds three refusals on the table, each exiting 2 and naming the call that does what the person meant. A verb that reads no words (version, rpc, acp, doctor, gc, serve, console) refuses trailing ones, and `undo` takes only `list`: `yi undo the last edit` used to restore the last turn's files and ignore the sentence, and now prints ``error: `yi undo` takes only `list`; to ask the model: yi ask undo the last edit``. A single word that is not a verb but sits one edit from one (four or five letters; two edits from six letters on; never below four) is refused with `error: did you mean "doctor"? to ask the model: yi ask doctr`, where `yi doctr` used to pay a model turn; two or more words, or a word far from every verb, still ask, and on a terminal still open the TUI. `yi console` (bare `yi` on a terminal included) and `yi serve` refuse `--thinking`, `--system`, `--schema` and `--deadline`, which they never hand a session (`serve_flags` forwards only the model, the session directory and the mode), naming the config key or the verb that reads it; a headless console drive still takes `--deadline`, which it reads. docs/YI_DESIGN.md section 17.1's first row now says so. Refs #1270, #1273. (D469, D470, D471; Closes #1468, Refs #1442) |
| 0.746.0 | 2026-10-25 | A spawn's `check` now meets the gate an armed command does. The check runs `sh -c` on the host when the parent calls `rlm.result`, outside the sandbox, and until now nothing judged it: a user's gate rule that would have stopped the same command in a bash call, an `exec://` heartbeat source or an armed todo let it through. The host gets the gate the wiring already builds for heartbeat sources (`tools::heartbeat_gate`, renamed `host_gate` since it now serves both), so the check passes the user's gate rules, the wall's command-text check and walled roots, then the broker's decision on the bash call it amounts to. It is judged twice. `rlm.run` refuses a denied check at spawn, before anything is admitted, with the gate's own reason: `the check is refused before it runs on the host: Denied by rule ...`. `rlm.result` judges it again just before it runs, because a rule can be promoted between the two, and holds the result back with `result held back; its check is refused: ...`. That second judgment counts no new attempt (`RuleEngine::recheck_tool`): a rule that counted the check at spawn reads that count, and one promoted since counts it as its first, so a gate with `after: 2` is not met by one spawn and its result. An allowed check still runs in the child's cwd, and a red one still holds the result back with its evidence. The three refusal sentences the gate shares with heartbeat sources no longer say `an exec:// source`, since a spawn check meets them too; they name the command and say it runs later on the host, and the contained one's remedy reads `run it yourself with bash, or allow it by a permission rule`. In a sandboxed Auto session a check the classifier does not allow outright (`pytest -q`, `make test`, `touch x`) is now refused at spawn with that sentence, as an armed todo command already is, until a permission rule allows it. A gate rule keeps its informed retry: with `gap: once` the same command passes the second time, as it does for a bash call (#1046 stays open for that decision). One feature-ledger row (trigger rules) names the new journey. (Closes #1055, Refs #1442) |
| 0.745.0 | 2026-10-25 | A root child that cannot spawn is no longer registered for the orchestrate protocol. `ExtOptions` gains `can_delegate`; `ext::install` registers the `orchestrate` extension only when it is true. The root (`session_extensions` in the CLI) passes true, and `child_factory` passes `depth + 1 < max_depth`, which mirrors the depth refusal in `SubagentHost::spawn`. At the default `max_depth` of 1 every root child is a leaf, so a brief that tripped the trigger (the word "orchestrate", or a prompt the router scores Complex) used to attach the 11,222-byte protocol to a session that could never spawn. A leaf's bare first request falls from 57,052 B to 45,561 B when its brief triggers the protocol, a drop of 11,491 B; an untriggered leaf was already 45,561 B and is unchanged. The router's `route` record for a leaf's prompt is no longer written, because the extension that wrote it is not registered. The same extension's one nudge goes too: a leaf no longer reads "This task has outgrown one-shot handling; write the plan now.", which the protocol sent after an edit to a file not yet read and after a failed check that followed an edit. The plan tool stays available to a leaf as a todo list. A fork gets no exemption. The strategy kept the protocol for a `fork=all` child only if its request prefix were its parent's, which would let the fork share the parent's cache. Measured at this head with a `yi ask` root and a `fork=all` root child: the system blocks differ (32,803 B against 30,709 B, first difference at byte 29,739, where the root's unattended fragment sits) and the tool tables differ (17,673 B against 15,181 B), and the tool table comes first in a request, so the fork shares no prefix with its parent whether or not the protocol is registered. A leaf root child is also no longer taught to delegate. The delegation teaching moved out of `doctrine.md` and `identity.md` into a new `prompts/delegation.md`, which `ext::install` attaches as the `delegation` slot at the Doctrine rank only when `can_delegate` is true. It holds the identity bullet on RLM subagents, the working model's intro and its rules on solo work, readers and writers, the reader wall, the flow, ownership and waiting, and data in kernels, with the closing `rlm.run` example, and Planning's first two sentences. The identity example now shows `rlm.ask(q, [url], schema=SCHEMA)`, the common case, instead of a full `role="root"` child. The kept text is the working model's "Bash or the kernel" rule, now under its own heading, and the rest of Planning (ground unknowns by exploring, never invent a schema). The delegation rules are renumbered one to five. A leaf's bare first request falls from 45,561 B to 41,738 B, a drop of 3,823 B; a leaf whose brief trips the orchestrate trigger falls from 57,052 B to 41,738 B in all. The root's prompt carries the same text in a new order and with two new headings, so its bare first request grows by 45 B (50,233 B to 50,278 B), and the request budget rises with it. The order within the Doctrine rank is by slot name, so `delegation` sits after the identity sections and before `# Operating doctrine`. A session resumed from an older snapshot restores its old prompt whole, which still carries the teaching inline. Three pointers to delegation stay in the text a leaf reads, left as known residue for a follow-up under #1442: Method step 4 (Ground) names "readers when the questions outnumber the turns", step 5 (Plan) lists "delegation" among the reasons to plan, and `identity.md` describes `plan` as "the delegation ledger ... for work you hand out". Each sits in a sentence that also carries text a leaf needs, so moving one rewrites the root's bytes as well; what they point at, the rules for readers and writers, is what moved. A leaf that follows one is refused with "RLM recursion depth limit reached", as it was before this change. Docs: YI_DESIGN section 6 (Prompt) says `ext::install` attaches `delegation` and registers `orchestrate` only for a session that can delegate, and section 11 (Child) says what a leaf is. Members: B1-leaf-orchestrate, B1-leaf-doctrine. (D467, D468; Closes #1465, Refs #1442) |
